> **@ralk7048**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=Ugxl99w5E4h0sqifYRB4AaABAg.AQJA7b9p5DwAQjwqF0LzSL)  
>
> ​@ricoeva0113 ​ I think people in this thread has argued that it's not entirely a contradiction. But Jiang himself touches on these topics so briefly that I agree it can be confusing.
  
> 
  
> For maths, we have:
  
> 1. school grade maths that force memorization of highly abstract concepts and makes you stupid because of the high cognitive load of working with something you don't understand - that's Jiang's analogy of the stack of building blocks with the middle part removed/becoming a black box
  
> 2. high-level pure mathematics that explore these concepts deeply enough for us to build from first principles - but even then Jiang argues the cognitive load is so high you can only be a great mathematician when you're in your 20s and 30s, the brain can't keep up later on (British mathematician G. H. Hardy had lamented in the famous book Mathematician's Apology that in his 60s he lost his creativity in maths compared to his younger years)  
>   
> Jiang did say (school) maths makes you stupid, but in one of his Substack posts, he recommended the book/course Nand2Tetris, a foundational Computer Science book that builds a computer from the ground up from logic gates all the way to a computer with a working OS, as a way to understand why he believes the AI fearmongering is a farce. I'd argue that Computer Science itself is quite abstract and related to maths, but the book gives enough details and starts from a low enough level of computing first principles that it won't entirely fit Jiang's "stupid maths" category.  
>   
> As for learning and teaching, that goes into the neuropsychology of learning.   
>   
> To learn things well, you need to approach it in a multimedia kind of way, look at it from every angle, seek understanding and not rote-memorization. We used to have a thing about "learning styles", like visual or auditory or kinesthetic, but that's proven to be false by research - it helps to do all 3. You also should understand how your own thinking works when you learn - so, learning how to learn.   
> An example is learning a new language; there are a lot of methods out there, and if you try them out, you'll see that a lot of them take advantage of how we learn and think (like putting words in context, etc.).  
>   
> A good way to learn is to teach, and it is also a functional measure of the mastery of what you learned; to teach it well, you're forced to dissect and summarize the knowledge and encode it in your brain using your own methods. By doing this kind of knowledge recall, you're also forming and strengthening connections between the maps of memory inside your brain.   
>   
> By having such a connected knowledge base, you can draw on many disciplines of knowledge, see your society through economics, psychology, science, philosophy, linguistics, music... whatever you can systematize and connect through your own experiences. As you learn and make your learning better by teaching, you'll be able to make cross-discipline connections to see things using different lenses, and become wise.   
> For example, Jiang now has 2 different lecture series on his channel, one on "Civilization" and one on "Secret Societies". It's the same history content, but he's teaching and thinking and exploring with a different lens - in Civilization, the ideas are more scattered, but he emphasizes Peter Turchin's concept of Elite Overproduction; in Secret Societies, he does mention this too, but he has a much more religious lean, and skips over history parts that aren't as relevant.  
>   
> And ironically, I realise that his idea that "maths makes you stupid" in itself is a rather abstract concept because you're required to hold contradictory ideas in your head about maths and education.   
> This goes back to psychology theories of brain development, like Piaget's - children tend to think in concrete yes/no, while in adolescence we start to be able to comprehend abstract concepts and hold contradictions, which shows that abstraction is a higher-level use of the brain. As the word higher-level implies, it builds on the underlying foundations of thinking, which is why missing-in-the-middle school education breaks your brain.  
> An example of abstraction is the ability to think in 1st person (everything happens to you), then 2nd person (understand the other), and then 3rd person (compare, empathy), and then make the jump to thinking about your own thinking (meta-cognition).  
>   
> Wall of text, but that's how I make sense of it.

> Pinpointing the exact families with direct, conspicuous links from historical European royalty or the Church (both Catholic and Protestant) to today's wealthiest corporations and hedge funds is challenging due to the reasons mentioned in our previous discussion: the complexity of financial structures, the passage of time, and the private nature of wealth.  
>   
> However, we can identify some prominent European families with historical power and influence, and note where their descendants or related entities are still significant players in modern finance and industry. It's important to understand that the nature of their involvement may have evolved considerably.  
>   
> Here are a few examples, keeping in mind that the connections can be intricate and not always direct:  
> Families with Historical Royal or Noble Ties:  
>  * The House of Liechtenstein: The Princely Family of Liechtenstein remains one of Europe's wealthiest royal families. Their wealth is largely attributed to their private bank, LGT Group, which manages significant assets. While their historical power was as rulers, their present-day influence is strongly tied to finance.  
>  * The Wallenberg Family (Sweden): While not royalty, the Wallenberg family has been a dominant force in Swedish industry and finance for over a century. They have significant holdings in major corporations like Ericsson, ABB, and AstraZeneca through their investment companies. Their historical influence stemmed from their early banking activities and strategic industrial investments.  
>   
>  * Certain aristocratic families in the UK: Some families with long-standing noble titles and historical land ownership have diversified their assets over time, with descendants involved in finance, real estate, and other business ventures. Identifying specific hedge fund affiliations can be difficult due to privacy.  
> Families with Historical Ties to the Catholic Church (often through banking or land ownership):  
>  * The Fugger Family (Germany): This family was a major banking and mercantile power in the 15th and 16th centuries, even financing Holy Roman Emperors. While their direct influence in modern finance isn't as dominant as in the past, they still exist and manage significant assets, including real estate and forestry. Their historical connection to the Church was through their role as financiers.  
>  * The Rothschild Family (various European branches): While Jewish, the Rothschild family rose to prominence in European banking in the late 18th and 19th centuries and had financial dealings with the Holy See.   
> See  Rothschild & Co.  
>  * Torlonia Family (Italy): This family rose to prominence in Rome through banking in the 18th and 19th centuries and had close financial ties to the Vatican. While their direct influence in global hedge funds might not be widely known, they remain a significant landowning family in Italy with diverse business interests.  
> Families with Historical Ties to Protestantism (often through mercantile activities and evolving social influence):  
>  * Certain Dutch and British families: The rise of Protestantism in these regions coincided with significant mercantile expansion. Some families who accumulated wealth through trade and shipping during these periods have evolved into influential players in modern finance and industry. Again, specific hedge fund links are hard to pinpoint publicly.  
> Important Considerations:  
>  * Evolution of Wealth: The source of wealth has changed dramatically. Land ownership and traditional industries have often given way to finance, technology, and global commerce.  
>  * Diversification: Wealthy families often diversify their holdings across various sectors, including corporations, real estate, and financial instruments like hedge funds.  
>  * Privacy: The ultra-wealthy often prioritize privacy, making it difficult to trace their exact affiliations.  
>   
>  * Indirect Influence: Influence can be exerted through ownership stakes, board memberships, philanthropic activities, and social networks, rather than direct control.  
>   
> It's more accurate to speak of a legacy of influence and wealth concentration among certain European families with historical prominence, rather than direct, easily traceable links to a specific list of 3,000 individuals and specific hedge funds. The connections are often complex, indirect, and part of a broader tapestry of evolving economic and social power.

>
> @David-xd6hiI like Math a lot and it’s my favorite subject in school, but I can totally understand where the prof is coming from. I didn’t take it as Math is not useful or anything, but more of a commentary on the general idea of math and how some people use math to explain the universe. Math is a man-made construct that probably came about for practical purposes. Just like language, it’s made to be a tool to facilitate solving problems and creating other tools. In fact, the prof is using one of the byproducts of math being used as a tool (the fancy white board) and even in the prediction model he uses. The problem is when people think of math as some sort of law that can explain everything. Also, a lot of math nerds study math purely in theory which makes it useless. This is exactly why even though I loved Math, I decided to pursue something that involves applying it in the real world (engineering). Even prof’s example of memorizing formulas don’t fly with me, because to me, if you had to memorize formulas, then you’re not really good at math and I doubt you truly understand what it’s meant for.  
>   
> EDIT: to add, I’m very grateful that we had to take other non-technical classes in university as well to keep us well-rounded. I understand prof’s criticisms about universities, but thankfully, I got the best of what I could get back then. It probably helped that I didn’t live in the west.

> @jg3000 Ah, here we have a point of confusion. "Mathematical proof" is a "term of art" to a mathematician - none of what you have described are even remotely mathematical proof. But I guess if you go more colloquial, where "proof" means "lots of evidence" and "mathematical" means "using maths" then... your words make sense. I don't accept it, though - as I don't accept the reuse of important terms in general to mean something totally different. A "mathematical proof" is a truth that follows from the axioms by way of pure logical and mathematics. If the proof was correct then the result is never wrong. It is impossible to "mathematically prove" anything about the real world without including a premise about the real world - and then the proof merely shows that "Assuming X then Y". Mathematical proof can produce tools that can be applied to the real world, but that's as close as it comes - it doesn't risk being wrong.

### **@Carla.M_1** | 👍 132 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=Ugw7Y7wbsQfS862mGop4AaABAg)

‼️‼️‼️ Professor. I'm a PhD mathematician, a postdoc who gave up pursuing professorship due to personal reasons. You're *wrong* about math being about memorizing. It's actually completely the opposite. Since I started doing maths, I started to forget everything and need to write everything down. Because I've learned to recall information via little clues, instead of remembering everything.

Mathematics DOES teach you reasoning. But not the reasoning that you're used to. You're used to reason "from front to back". Mathematics reasons "from back to front", mostly (there's rare exceptions where the other way round is needed).
I remember when I started doing math how my brain was literally rewired to reason "backward".

In logics that is taught in philosophy, however, the reasoning is the the "proper way round".

Anyway. I can 100% guarantee you that real math is definitely about reasoning. It may be a different form of reasoning that people are not used to. But it is definitely and 100% a form of reasoning. Math is NOT about formulas. Math is about patterns.

And yes, mathematical intuition is based on "images"/ strong imagination. If a mathematician can't imagine the structures they work with as "pictures", they imho will not be able to finish their PhD.

Btw - no, mathematicians are not stupid. They are as stupid as predictive history people. There are some stupid and some intelligent.

Mathematics is not satanic 🙄 it's a tool. You can use it for good, you can use it for bad. But mostly it's so abstract that you can use it only thousands of years later 😉

Regarding mathematical objects, we call them objects because we chose this term arbitrarilly, doesn't mean their ontology is the same as an apple or a chair. That's why i mentioned most mathematicians are platonists because they actually believe mathematical objects have a stand alone existence and i think the strongest argument is Quine-Putnam's "unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics" which is constructed from its relationship with science. But i'm not in this camp,  from my pov math is a language, an instrument constructed by our cognitive apparatus as a way to interpret a particular slce of reality. My methaphysics are more akin to Kant's as you might have noticed. But i'm very aware of the amazing power of math to f*** with someones mind in such a way that it makes you extremely anthropocentric in your further interpretation of the world in the sense that you tend to instantly believe you found a universal framework when in fact is just how our cognitive apparatus filters and intreprets the external world.   
>   
> I know it sounds very salad wordy and sophisticated (because it is practically) but what analogy helped me escape that framework when i was younger and i was fully invested in this whole materialistic, scientistic, cartesian worldview was thinking about the light spectrum. The fact that all  the light, the combination of colors available to our human cognitive aprehension is only a tiny slice of the spectrum we call "visible light", while there are so many types of "light" with different lenghtwaves we can't directly see because our human limits, like gamma rays or at the other extreme, radio waves, ingraiined the idea of human limit in me and made me very skeptic over the years with our capabilities to grasp UNIVERSAL stuff let's say (like laws, objects,  patterns, etc). Use this analogy in relation to our capacity to grasp universality when you think about this very consequential statement like "math is the language of the universe". > @Carla.M_1 It's kind of difficult to answer since I only have my own personal experience to share, but I've arrived at some logical conclusions that are currently being validated by studies (this is kind of a scientific frontier right now; there are few studies on this topic).  
>   
> The first thing to mention is that imagination is multi-layered, and each layer has a full spectrum of possibilities. Yes, most people have roughly the same imagination capabilities, but this doesn't mean we are all equal. Some people can imagine smells, voices, details, or colors better than others. For example, around 5% of people have hyperphantasia, which means they have the ability to create extremely vivid mental imagery.  
>   
> In my case, I wouldn't say I have a 'bad' imagination per se; for example, I can reproduce voices clearly, but I struggle with visual representation. This difference also affects episodic foresight, meaning I tend to plan the future using logic and facts rather than by "pre-living" vivid mental scenarios. This logical approach often correlates with an inclination toward STEM fields, while those with hyperphantasia often lean toward the Arts.  
>   
> That being said, I would argue that I generally live a normal life, but it can affect how you experience emotions and their intensity. For instance, people with aphantasia can recall both good and bad memories (such as trauma), and when they reproduce those feelings, they do actually feel them. However, for me, feelings can be pretty intense at the peak moment, but afterwards, they just dilute over time, and I can't bring them back that easily.

It's nonsense that just redefines "reality" as shared perception and ignores that the world would still exist without us. That's not to say that thinkers like Faucault didn't make good observations about how our concepts are socially situated and contingent on an entire history of social evolution, but some thinkers in this vein seem to attempt to collapse reality into just our perception of it.

1:44:20 the reason the Templars were destroyed had nothing to do with theological differences, it was because King Philip IV of France wanted to take all their wealth for himself and falsely accused them of "denying Christ and trampling on the Cross" during their initiations, stripping initiates naked during initiation and kissing them on the navel, posterior, and lips, indulging in "unnatural lust" (read: homosexuality), worshiping an idol of Baphomet and using it to consecrate the cord worn by neophyte members of the order, and not consecrating the host during mass. It is notable that Philip attempted to levy these exact same charges against Pope Boniface XVIII previously.

Under pressure from Philip, Pope Clement V did arrest all the knights templars but insisted on their right to a fair trial. While Philip got several confessions through use of torture and burnt many templars at the stake, the Papal Council investigating the templars found no evidence of the heresy of which they were accused, and they finally abolished the Templar Order, not on criminal grounds, but in order to avoid angering Philip and "maintaining the general welfare of the Church".

For someone who claims not to be a conspiracy theorist, you're repeating a lot of easily disproven conspiracy theory talking points.

### **@mortysmith999**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=Ugw7O9hjgqe7G_JEjLx4AaABAg)

The history of the Catholic Church reveals its rise to power in Europe, especially after Constantine made Christianity the official religion. The Church, while promoting unity, faced internal and external conflicts, resulting in wars and the eventual split between Catholics and Protestants, culminating in the Reformation.



Highlights:

01:30 The transition from the Roman Empire to the Byzantine Empire was marked by corruption and stagnation of the oligarchy. Constantine's move of the capital was crucial for reinventing Roman society.

  - The Roman oligarchy, composed of patrician families, generated inequality and contributed to corruption. This system proved problematic as the empire expanded.

  - The mass migration of nomadic peoples from the East forced Rome to recruit mercenaries to protect its frontiers. This led to a new social dynamic between migrants and Roman nobles.

  - The division of the Roman Empire into eastern and western parts led to very different fates. The Byzantine Empire thrived due to its strategic location and wealth.



08:13 The Catholic Church became the primary spiritual authority in Western Europe, dominating both politics and religion. This rise generated significant conflicts among different societies and beliefs.

  - Western Europe's cultural diversity, due to natural barriers, resulted in competing societies. These distinct societies were shaped by their unique cultures and rivalries.

  - The Catholic Church implemented the Inquisition to control dissensions and ensure religious orthodoxy. This process led to internal conflicts and persecution of heretics.

  - Constantine made Christianity the official religion of Rome, seeking doctrinal uniformity. This provoked theological debates, especially about the nature of Jesus.



16:20 The debate about the nature of the three divine forces—God, Jesus, and the Holy Spirit—is complex and confusing. Various explanations, such as modalism, partialism, and Arianism, present conceptual problems.

  - Modalism suggests that God, Jesus, and the Holy Spirit are different modes of the same essence. This view implies they are interchangeable in their manifestations.

  - Partialism considers that God, Jesus, and the Holy Spirit are parts of the same divine force. This analogy is likened to an ocean with interconnected parts.

  - Arianism proposes that God is superior to Jesus, considering Him a later creation. This view challenges the central Christian idea that Jesus is God.



24:28 The video discusses how imposing religious orthodoxy and pressure to learn mathematics can lead to intellectual and creative exhaustion. It also explores the historical implications of this phenomenon in ancient societies.

  - The relationship between learning mathematics and creativity is explored, highlighting how it can lead to mental exhaustion in older individuals.

  - The video mentions historical conflicts between the Byzantine Empire and the Sassanids, which were exacerbated by the imposition of religious orthodoxies.

  - The rise of Arab society is discussed, emphasizing its role as a center of trade and cultural exchange during periods of conflict between great empires.



32:38 The central message is that God’s messengers always promote freedom and the divinity present in each individual. They do not seek to be followed as gods, but as spiritual guides.

  - The importance of God’s messengers is that they seek to liberate people from oppression such as slavery and poverty. This is a common message across different religions.

  - The transition from spiritual message to the formation of organized religions is a recurring process in history, where figures like Jesus and Muhammad end up institutionalized.

  - After the fall of the Roman Empire, various migrations and conflicts among nobles resulted in a variety of Christian beliefs in Europe, reflecting resistance to the official doctrine.



40:44 The idea of the Holy Trinity faced significant resistance in its early years, requiring wars for its imposition by the Iron Swan (note: original says “Império Bisonte”; likely a fictional or mistaken term). Over time, the acceptance and spread of Christianity grew, expanding beyond borders.

  - The Bisonte Empire, under Justinian, reached the height of its power in 565, but its fall was swift due to various internal and external pressures.

  - Christian resistance led many to seek refuge in the Persian Empire, where they were initially well received, but conflicts arose with Zoroastrians.

  - The desertification of Arabia became a crucial trading hub, connecting diverse cultures and facilitating the spread of religious ideas, such as Islam.



49:12 The Medina Constitution, announced by Muhammad, promised religious openness and tolerance for all, regardless of faith. This promise of unity was an important foundation for the Muslim community.

  - Muhammad emphasized the importance of religious tolerance, allowing Jews, Christians, and Arabs to practice their beliefs freely. This diversity was crucial for forming a united society.

  - The role of Jesus in Islam is addressed, highlighting that he is seen as a messenger of God, not God. This differentiates Islamic beliefs from Christian ones.

  - The rapid expansion of Islam after Muhammad’s death is attributed to the desire for religious liberation among the peoples. This expansion had a major impact on world history.



57:07 Islamic religion promotes trust among Muslims, enabling the creation of trade routes that generate global prosperity. This trust is foundational for unity and cooperation among believers.

  - Islam is regarded by its followers as the supreme religion, surpassing Judaism and Christianity in its view of community and morality.

  - After the fall of the Roman Empire, the Catholic Church becomes the main authority, regulating spiritual and material life in European society.

  - The economic growth of Europe between 1000 and 1300 makes the Catholic Church extremely rich, contrasting with Jesus’s teachings about wealth.



1:05:15 The Catholic Church had powerful and corrupt influence in medieval society, controlling faith and wealth. Its influence led to corrupt practices, such as selling indulgences and simony.

  - Church rituals were complex and often incomprehensible, keeping the faithful in a submissive position. This contributed to a lack of debate and questioning among members.

  - The relationship between the Church and the kings generated tensions, as the Church controlled a significant portion of lands and taxes. This led to revolts against the Church’s authority by monarchs.

  - The legitimacy of the Catholic Church was challenged by Muslim occupations and internal divisions, such as the schism with the Eastern Orthodox Church. This generated a crisis of faith and legitimacy among the faithful.



1:13:25 The Catholic Church faced legitimacy and power crises during the Middle Ages, resulting in internal conflicts and the rise of reform movements. These events culminated in the emergence of Protestantism and the decline of ecclesiastical control.

  - The Knights Templar acted as one of the first multinational banks, facilitating money transfers for pilgrims. This financial innovation contributed to the group’s wealth and influence.

  - The crusade against the Cathars was not only religious but also a geopolitical struggle, where local lords saw the Church as a threat to their power. This generated support for dissident movements.

  - The Inquisition was a Church response to control heresy and dissidence, leading to an atmosphere of fear and repression in Europe. This contributed to cultural and social stagnation of the time.



1:21:36 The placebo effect demonstrates how belief and perception can influence physical health. When we believe something can heal us, we often experience real improvements in our condition.

  - The loss of faith in oneself can be one of the causes of cancer, reflecting the importance of self-esteem and life purpose. Belief in oneself is fundamental.

  - The personal willingness to seek help, such as consulting a psychiatrist, can have a significant impact on recovery. Internal motivation is crucial for improvement.

  - Practices like meditation help control the mind and reduce stress, which can accelerate the healing process. Mental self-control is essential for health.

### **@NHindel**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=UgzlDGbfIZrPXoZHIk54AaABAg)

The lecture presents a sweeping narrative that attempts to explain the transformation of the Roman world, the rise of Christianity and Islam, and the structure of medieval Europe as a single interconnected system driven by political power, geography, religion, and migration. While the ambition to synthesize large-scale history is clear, the argument blends descriptive history with speculative interpretation, selective generalization, and several factual and conceptual distortions.

At a structural level, the lecture follows a deterministic and monocausal style: complex historical developments are reduced to a few overriding forces—oligarchy, bureaucracy, religious orthodoxy, and migration dynamics. For example, the Roman Republic is described as an oligarchy of a small number of families, which is broadly correct in spirit, but the lecture compresses centuries of institutional evolution into a static model. The transition from Republic to Empire is framed less as a series of contingent political crises and more as an inevitable consequence of oligarchic rigidity. This overlooks the roles of military competition, provincial governance, economic transformation, and charismatic leadership.

The portrayal of key figures such as Gaius Julius Caesar and Augustus is similarly simplified. Augustus’ establishment of the principate is characterized as a failed attempt at bureaucracy, whereas in historical scholarship it is often interpreted as a successful reconfiguration of republican institutions into a durable imperial system. The lecture frames bureaucracy as a necessary evolutionary stage that Rome “failed” to reach in the West, later achieved in the East (Byzantium), but this teleological model imposes a modern administrative ideal onto ancient societies without sufficient justification.

The discussion of the split between East and West, centered on Constantine the Great (Constantine the Great), contains both accurate elements and oversimplifications. The relocation of the capital to Constantinople and the increasing administrative focus on the eastern provinces are historically grounded. However, the claim that the Eastern Roman Empire became a “Greek bureaucracy” analogous to China is an interpretive analogy rather than a historically demonstrated equivalence. Such comparisons can be heuristically useful but are presented here as if they reflect structural identity rather than partial similarity.

A major methodological issue in the lecture is the use of broad civilizational generalizations, such as describing Byzantium and China as functionally similar bureaucratic empires, or Western Europe as inherently fragmented due to geography. While geography did influence political fragmentation in Europe, the argument neglects other factors such as feudal contractualism, the legacy of Roman law, and the decentralized nature of post-imperial authority. The lecture tends to treat geography as destiny rather than one variable among many.

The treatment of religious history is where the lecture becomes most interpretively driven and least historically balanced. The Council of Nicaea is accurately referenced as a turning point in doctrinal standardization, convened under Constantine, but the lecture frames doctrinal debates—such as Christology and Trinitarian theology—as largely incoherent or artificially imposed for political control. While it is true that theological disputes were complex and often politically entangled, mainstream historical theology does not regard formulations like the Trinity as purely arbitrary; rather, they emerged from sustained philosophical engagement with scriptural interpretation and Hellenistic metaphysics.

Figures such as Augustine of Hippo are presented primarily as architects of ecclesiastical power rather than as intellectuals responding to philosophical and theological problems of their time. This reduces the diversity of motivations and intellectual traditions within early Christianity.

A key limitation of the lecture is its largely materialist and power-centered interpretation of religion. It tends to treat belief systems mainly as instruments of control or political cohesion. This overlooks a crucial dimension: belief systems function as cognitive and symbolic infrastructures that make civilizations possible in the first place. Civilizations like Rome did not operate on coercion alone; they relied on shared meanings, moral frameworks, and intellectual traditions that enabled trust, coordination, and legitimacy across vast populations.

In this sense, religion and philosophy were not merely imposed from above but also internalized by societies and experienced as meaningful worldviews. Roman civic religion, imperial cult practices, and later Christian theology provided a shared symbolic language through which authority could be understood and accepted. Intellectual traditions—Greek philosophy, Roman law, and later scholastic theology—also structured how reality, justice, and human purpose were conceptualized.

A more balanced interpretation, therefore, is that civilization emerges from the interaction between material organization and symbolic meaning. Economic surplus, military structures, and geography provide the conditions for large-scale societies, but belief systems supply the coherence that binds those structures together. Without such frameworks, coordination, legitimacy, and continuity over generations would be far more difficult to sustain.

The lecture’s discussion of mathematics and education similarly reflects a broader skepticism toward formal systems, suggesting that memorization-based learning diminishes critical thinking. While there is a valid critique of rote learning when it is isolated from understanding, the characterization of mathematics as inherently intellectually degrading is not supported by academic perspectives. Mathematics is generally understood as a discipline that develops abstraction, logical reasoning, and problem-solving capacity rather than suppressing them.

The sections on Islam and the rise of Muhammad (Muhammad) emphasize themes of unification and tolerance. While early Islamic history does involve consolidation of tribal groups and expansion into surrounding regions, the lecture reduces a complex historical and theological development into a single unifying narrative centered on tolerance and political exhaustion of neighboring empires. This simplifies the diversity of early Islamic jurisprudence, theology, and political evolution.

Another recurring issue is the conflation of narrative storytelling with explanatory causation. For instance, migrations from the Eurasian steppe, the decline of Byzantium, and the rise of Islam are linked in a linear chain of cause and effect. While these factors are historically connected, the lecture presents them as part of a single directional process rather than a set of overlapping, contingent developments influenced by multiple independent variables.

Overall, the lecture functions less as a conventional academic history and more as a synthetic interpretive narrative that organizes historical events around themes of power, control, orthodoxy, and systemic transformation. It contains elements of historical fact but embeds them within a deterministic and sometimes reductionist framework.

A more complete analysis must recognize not only the material and political dimensions emphasized in the lecture, but also the intellectual and spiritual dimensions that underlie them. Civilizations endure not only because of structures of coercion or administration, but because they are sustained by shared belief systems that provide meaning, legitimacy, and a framework for understanding reality. Ignoring this dimension leads to an incomplete account; overemphasizing it leads to mystification. A balanced historiography must hold both together.

And our problem is that scene in All Quiet on the Western Front where the schoolboys march off dutifully in a line to war. Our problem is that people are obedient all over the world, in the face of poverty and starvation and stupidity, and war and cruelty. Our problem is that people are obedient while the jails are full of petty thieves, and all the while the grand thieves are running the country. That’s our problem.”

In Arabic, "Islam" (إسلام) literally means
"submission to God" and originates from the root word salama (س-ل-م), which also relates to concepts of peace. The word "Muslim" (مُسْلِم), meaning a follower of Islam, is derived from the same root and translates to "one who submits" or "submitter to God". 

    Submission: The primary religious meaning of "Islam" is the total submission and surrender to the will of God (Allah).
    Peace: The word is also connected to "salam" (سلام), which means peace. A common interpretation is that personal peace is achieved through total submission to God.
    Root word: The word comes from a root that connects concepts of submission, peace, and safeness. For example, the word saleem (سليم) means safe or sound

Hello Dr. Jiang, I would like to say that I both disagree and agree on your evaluation of mathematics. Math is not only related to, but is derived from reality by using a consistent procedure to evaluate 'things', and looking at every single possibility, only arriving on an answer when there are no other possibilities left. When done properly theoretically, math builds a complete (at least mostly) structure from bottom to top which is shown in the proof. However, the way that math is taught in schools and often the way it is used practically there are major jumps and gaps in the reasoning, as much of math is too largely abstracted and difficult to understand, most people never look at proofs. Especially with the way that it is taught with the excessive focus on 'getting the right answer' rather than the procedure itself, many never bridge that gap between reality and what they are learning. So, my point is rather than math being stupid, people are stupid.

Sigh… ^_^ Sometimes I wonder if there’s really much difference anymore between churches, corporations, and even governments. Each one becomes a kind of structure built by people — and people carry the same desires, fears, ambitions, and blind spots wherever they go. Maybe the labels change, but the human behavior inside them feels strangely familiar across all three.

When everything can be organized, branded, and sold, the question of “right or wrong” starts to blur — especially when objects, symbols, and even beliefs get produced far away, almost out of thin air, and then passed back to us as something sacred or essential.

And that part in the video about water still lingers in my mind. It makes me curious: what did Earth’s water look like at the beginning? Was it perfectly clear, untouched by life? If the sky mirrors the sea, then was the early sky almost invisible too? It’s fascinating how the world we see may only be a reflection of the world beneath it.

Even the red moon feels like a reminder of how perception changes with light and circumstance. Sometimes I imagine… if the oceans ever lost their life, would the sky shift with them? Would the world reflect that loss in color?

These videos always leave me with more questions than answers — but maybe that’s the beauty of it. ^_^

As an educator, Professor Jiang offers some thought-provoking ideas on history and patterns of power. However, please approach his content with caution, especially younger viewers. In his recent video praising Stalin as “the greatest man who ever lived” and an “übermensch,” he justifies the Great Purge and other atrocities as necessary “pruning of dead weight” and barely mentions the tens of millions who died under Stalin’s policies or Mao’s Great Leap Forward and Cultural Revolution.

52:20 what are you talking about? The Nestorians are not Arians, they believe that Jesus is God. The Nestorian split happened because they argued that Mary couldn't be called the Mother of God, just the mother of Christ, because she was only the mother to Jesus's human nature, not his divine nature which is eternal. This was rejected by the Catholic and Orthodox Churches because it "separates" Christ's two natures, but Jesus can only "redeem" human nature if he fully took on human nature and united human nature with divine nature.

The Nestorian position, in the Catholic/Orthodox view, seems to keep Jesus's two natures as completely separate and disunited, and since in the Catholic/Orthodox view Jesus is the embodiment of the Platonic Ideal of Man and the Platonic Ideal of God, the unity which being what allows Christ to act as a bridge which unifies God and Man and redeems humanity, the Nestorian position is seen as a complete negation of the possibility of this redemption by keeping the two natures of Christ separate.

In other words, if Christ's two natures are united and he can take on humanity's "karma" onto himself because himself is Man with a capital M, then he can pay the price for us and act as the bridge between human nature and God, thus allowing us to transcend the limits of our nature and become like God, AKA "God became Man so that Man could become God". Since Jesus's two natures are united in this view, Mary must by logical necessity be the "Mother of God", and the Nestorian denial of this point is seen as tantamount to the denial of the unity of Christ's double nature and thus the denial of the very method via which salvation is possible in the Catholic/Orthodox view.

On the other hand, in the Nestorian view, calling Mary, a human woman, "The Mother of God" is an act of blasphemy because no human could ever be the parent of the divine because nothing can give birth to something of a different nature than it (evolution might have something to say about that but 431 AD was quite a bit away from Darwin's "On the Origin of Species").

> - *The St. Bartholomew's Day Massacre (1572)*: Catholic mobs killed thousands of Huguenots (French Protestants) in Paris and other cities, leading to a wave of violence across France.  
> - *The French Wars of Religion (1562-1598)*: A series of conflicts between Catholics and Huguenots, resulting in hundreds of thousands of deaths.  
> - *The Thirty Years War (1618-1648)*: A devastating conflict in Europe involving Catholics and Protestants, with estimated deaths ranging from 4.5 to 8 million.  
> - *The Spanish Inquisition (1478-1834)*: A tribunal established to root out heresy, resulting in the persecution and execution of thousands, including Protestants, Jews, and Muslims.  
> - *The Massacre of the Latins (1182)*: A massacre of Roman Catholics in Constantinople, resulting in an estimated 60,000 deaths.  
> - *The Waldensian Massacres (1488, 1655)*: Attacks on the Waldensian community in Italy, deemed heretical by the Catholic Church.  
> - *The Irish Rebellion (1641-1653)*: A conflict involving Catholics and Protestants, resulting in significant violence and displacement ¹ ² ³.

### **@materialknight**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=Ugy45lF4TMP77I4_PWR4AaABAg)

Nestorians didn't think Jesus was merely human, they thought Jesus was 2 people in one body: one human, one divine, so for them Mary was the mother of Jesus the human but not Jesus the God. Seen carefully, it isn't any easier to accept than the catholic dogma: That Jesus is one person with 2 natures/wills: one human (for human things), one divine (for miracles).

Paul didn't talk with the Holy Spirit, he talked with Jesus. The Holy Spirit is God-within-us, for example, when a non-Jesus person does a miracle, or speaks/writes what God wants, that is the Holy Spirit's doing.

Small clarification: It is catholic dogma that the Father is the ultimate source of everything and gives everything to Jesus, who is one with Him, but eternally begotten, without beginning or end (imagine a glass [Jesus] of an eternal lantern [God] that receives all the light and heat [power and Holy Spirit] of the flame [the Father] for all eternity), and equal in power. Arrianism just proposed that Jesus was created, instead of eternally there to begin with, and therefore not one with the Father, and less powerful.

The Trinity is not logical, but it wasn't made up at the council, the concept is in the New Testament from the 1st century, very clearly in some verses, although generally not as clearly because you risked being condemned by the jews for blasphemy, so it was a secret doctrine at the beginning, but well known by bishops, that's why the majority agreed on the dogma, and Constantine didn't make up the doctrine either, because he was an arrian, and when the council didn't have the result he desired, he persecuted the Church.

In the chart around minute 58, one of Christianity's weakness is "distant divinity", but God is literally a man, who suffered and died just like us, and He sends the Holy Spirit (which is God-within-us) at will, through sacraments, etc. so God can inhabit ourselves. How much closer a divinity can be?

Non-catholics stumble because they don't realize that Jesus, besides parables, also used hyperboles (like in Luke 14, 26, which doesn't mean you should hate everyone, it means that you must be willing to choose Him over any other authority, and over your own wishes, in case of contradiction).

Matthew 19,  23-24 is a hyperbole, because there were wealthy people following or assisting Jesus: Joseph of Arimathea, Nicodemus, Matthew, Zacchaeus, etc. Those verses must be interpreted in light of the preceding verses, Matthew 5, 3: "Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven", and 2 parables: the parable of the servants (Luke 12, 41-48) and the parable of the unforgiving servant (Mateo 18, 23-35), both of which talk about the Purgatory.

To begin with, in the verses preceding Matthew 19,  23, Jesus told the rich young man that he was saved already, but if he wanted to be PERFECT, he had to give everything to the poor. Most possibly Jesus was just testing him (like He tested the woman in Matthew 15, 21-28), because what is wrong is not to be rich, but being attached to riches. If the young man agreed to give up everything, he would have demonstrated his poverty of spirit (his craving need for God over anything else), but since he didn't, that means he was spiritually satisfied by his material wealth, which reveals an undue attachment to riches that needs to be purged in Purgatory, hence why a rich man cannot enter Heaven IMMEDIATELY, while he is attached to his riches, because no less than a saint can enter Heaven (Revelation 21, 27).

So Muhammad is not the Messiah figure in Islamic Tradition, and the Muslims believe Jesus to be the Messiah and his second coming.  And he will return according to several authentic Hadith (Sayings of the prophet). So that's the Muslim tradition belief of Jesus (the Messiah) and Muhammad (PBUH) as the final messenger of God.

### **@stashajovanovic623** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=Ugz_jFUIkVGn98O-NjF4AaABAg)

Saying “math makes you stupid” is going to far. I am more inclined to believe that Prof. did not go very far with his math, which is quite ok, and in fact logical, as it would be hard to believe he went this far, as he did with history, literature, philosophy, in so many fields, and also with math. That would make him exactly an intellectual Übermensch, which would in fact be suspicious. Math is simply difficult, when you pass certain level, but no different from, say, playing piano, or even better violin, or even tennis. There are sports that anyone could seem to be able to play even without any prior knowledge/training, e.g. badminton. Of course, you wouldn’t be able to play properly according to all the rules, but you would be able to hit a shuttle and get it over the net, and it would resemble a game, it would seem as if you are playing. With tennis, one just can’t play without any prior knowledge/training if someone on the other side of the court sends a “normal” ball, like between two players who know how to play. You wouldn’t even see the ball, let alone hit it. The same is with piano, or better violin. It takes a lot of time until you start producing tones bearable for people to listen, and it takes much more time to start really playing. The same is with math, especially higher levels of math. It takes a lot of time and effort (talent helps, but primarily to reduce the necessary time and effort) to understand it. You got a lot of tools that you need to learn how to use in order to produce a result. You need to joggle them, and each and every one of these tools is difficult to understand and learn how to use, and yet, you need to learn how to joggle them all at the same time. That is simply hard. But still, those people who get to the point to master them, they indeed can experience the ta-da moment, similar to the one in Matrix when Neo starts seeing the code underneath the whole world and all items in it, and starts being able to manipulate them at his will. And, even though it is true that historically many top breakthroughs were made before age 30, especially in pure, abstract areas, it is not universally true, and the pattern is weaker today. But, even if it were to be true, the explanation would be that simply math, being as complex as it is, it requires human brain at its best, at its cognitive peak, when its most energetic with maximum of its synapses firing at their best, and that is at the young age. Also, historically, breakthroughs were happening when people’s lives were shorter, whereas today, when math has gotten extremely vast and complex, as with all other fields, it is close to impossible to know everything. Instead, people, i.e. young scientists, need to choose a much narrower field in order to reach the final frontier of the existing knowledge, to be able to attempt to make a contribution and move this final frontier by some small iota. As in any other field, these shifts are done by young researchers guided by their more experienced older colleagues. It’s just the way the modern science works, as it is simply most efficient system, nothing else. It doesn’t mean, however, that breakthroughs are not made, even nowadays, by older scientists. They are, but it is just that the younger researchers are by far more numerous, and, in this case, quantity indeed berths quality more often, that is all. Let alone the fact that math teaches one how to use logic at it's best ...

SO IMPORTANT FOR UNDERSTANDING THE TRUTH
Rather than a widespread, organized, and society-wide expectation of a “Messiah” encompassing the entire Arab society, one can speak of a strong expectation within certain Jewish communities and a limited, primarily theological expectation within some Christian circles.
Jewish and Christian communities that held messianic expectations rejected Prophet Muhammad (PBUH) primarily because he did not conform to their respective definitions of the Messiah.
Among Jews, the most decisive reason for this rejection was his Arab/Ishmaelite origin, while among Christians, the principal reason was the belief that the Messiah was already Jesus (PBUH).
Among the Arabs themselves, however, no such belief existed, as they were predominantly polytheists. Therefore, the claim that Islam emerged as a continuation or fulfillment of an expected messianic savior figure does not reflect historical reality.
Islam is not a system that was established after the death of Prophet Muhammad (PBUH); rather, it is a divinely revealed way of life that was lived and exemplified by Prophet Muhammad (PBUH) himself.
Prophet Muhammad (PBUH) represented the highest and most noble model of human conduct; he was by no means merely an ordinary human being, as is sometimes claimed.
Furthermore, Islam spread across vast geographical regions primarily under Turkish political authority rather than Arab dominance, and it is not confined to any single ethnicity but encompasses all of humanity as the one true universal religion.

### **@volkerschmitz121**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TEdVhyE8pO0&lc=UgyxGX_gl6JBSWiZc7Z4AaABAg)

I really love Prof Jiang and I learned a lot about history. But what went wrong in this lecture.  "When you study math you become stupid..."....This is so obviously wrong! Only a person who has no clue about the art of math and also its role in all the good things we have can say something like this. Was this an aprils fool? Prof Jiang would not have a single subscriber without using all the tech which is all math based. And I think it is very good that he has this very large reach. Math is powerful and makes Prof Jiang a better person (helps his important work). Math is not bad because it can be misused. A knife is not bad, it is a tool. Can be used to work (butcher), for art and also to harm. Math is one of the best tools in the world to train focus and if one is more advanced it can be super creative. All modern movies would not be there without math. And yes there are good and bad movies from an artistical standpoint.  Math also was and is a super important part of philosophy (Logic). Math is every where! And the people who developed it were either good or bad people like everybody else,  this has nothing to do with math itself. And Prof Jiangs claim about age is just plain false (and makes my head shake in disbelief). A few examples of mathematicans who had break throughs after 40:
         
Andrew Wiles – Proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem (age 41)
Yitang Zhang – Breakthrough on bounded prime gaps (age 58)
Benoît Mandelbrot – Fractal geometry (50s)
Leonhard Euler – Major works in analysis and number theory (after 40)
Pierre-Simon Laplace – Mécanique céleste (around 50)
Joseph Fourier – Fourier series and heat equation (40–55)
Karen Uhlenbeck – Fundamental work in geometric analysis (after 40)
William Thurston – Geometrization program (after 40)
John Nash – Important PDE results (after 50)
Pierre de Fermat – Key number theory results in his 40s

And here are super important philosophers who where mathematicians as well:

1. **Pythagoras**
   *Philosophy:* Reality is fundamentally numerical; harmony and order govern the cosmos and ethics.

2. **Plato**
   *Philosophy:* Theory of Forms; knowledge as access to eternal, non-material truths beyond sensory experience.

3. **René Descartes**
   *Philosophy:* Rationalism and mind–body dualism; certainty grounded in self-conscious thought (*cogito*).

4. **Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz**
   *Philosophy:* Monadology; reality as composed of simple substances governed by pre-established harmony.

5. **Blaise Pascal**
   *Philosophy:* Existential tension between reason and faith; limits of rationality (*Pascal’s Wager*).

6. **Baruch Spinoza**
   *Philosophy:* Substance monism; God and Nature are identical; ethics based on necessity, not free will.

7. **Bertrand Russell**
   *Philosophy:* Analytic philosophy; logical analysis as the method of philosophy; critiques of metaphysics.

8. **Alfred North Whitehead**
   *Philosophy:* Process philosophy; reality as becoming rather than static being.

9. **Edmund Husserl**
   *Philosophy:* Founder of phenomenology; consciousness and intentionality as the basis of meaning.

10. **Kurt Gödel**
    *Philosophy:* Mathematical Platonism; arguments for the objectivity of truth beyond formal systems.

Philosophy with all those wise and old philosophers would be absolutely nowhere without math. I mean look at the list. Was that a test, if anybody in your class would stand up for truth??? Unbeliveable.


Pls consider taking back this awful comment --  Math is good! People may be bad. But to blame it on math is may I say: stupid.
